DISTILLATION.

SISTILLATION is the Art of fepa-
rating, or drawing off the fpirituous,
aqueous, and oleaginous Parts of 2

mixt Body from the groffer, and more ter-
teftrial Parts, by means of Fire, and con-

denfing them again by Cold,

We fhall therefore divide this 'lrestifé

arious Subftances ; in.the fecond, the man-
ner of drawing fimple Waters ; and in the
third, the beft Methods. of making cordisk
or compound Waters.

Of the Diftillation of Spirits. :

But as it is impoffible to extract vinous

is often neceffary, it will be requifite firft to
corifider thefe Operations.

CHAP. I

Of Brewing, in order to the Production of

CHAP. I. -

Of Fermentation, .- |

HE Tin&ure, or, as the Diftillers call
it, the Wath, being prepared, ‘asin -

the foregoing Chapter, it is next to be fet-

mented ; for, without this Operation, no
vinous Spiritcan be produced.  -

‘By Fermentation is meant that intefline
Motion performed by the inftrumental Effi-
cacy of Water, whereby the Salt, Oil and
Earth of a fermentable Subje&, are fepa-.
rated, attenuated, tranfpofed, and again
collected, and recompofed'in a particular
Manner. t

B 4 ; The
4 A Complete. Syftem

‘The Doctrine of Fermentation, 1 is of the
reateít Ufe, and fhould: be well ‘underftood
every Diftiller, as it is the very Bafis of
e Art; and, perhaps, if more attended

"" a nach purer Spirit, as well as à greater
Quantity of it, might | be procured from,
the fame Materials than. at prefent. - We
fhall therefore lay ‘down ‘a concife Theory
of Fermentation, before we procesd to de»
liver the Practice, " |

|. Every fermentable Subject i is ; compofed of
Salt, Oil, and.a fubtile Earth ; but thefe
Particles are fo "ímall, that, when afunder,
they are imperceptable to the Senfes ; and,
therefore, when mixed with an aqueous
laid, they leave. it. tranfparent ; neither
ave ermentable . Bodies. any Tafte, except
that of Sweetnefs,

 '(Thefe Particles are each compoíed "
Salt, Oil and Earth, intimately mixed in an
actual. Cohefion, Connexion, and Union ;
and, therefore,; when any one. of thofe
Principles, too much abounds in any Sub-,
ject,- fo thatan intimate Union is prevented,
the whole Efficacy of the Fermentation is
either flopped or. impaired, or at leaft Unis |
ted to one certain Species, ©

This

of Dittillation. T3

This equal Connexion of Salt, Oil and
Earth into a fingle compound Particle, forms
a Corpulcle foluble in Water ; or, to fpeak
more philofophically, this compound .Cor-
puícle is, by means of its faline Particles,
connected with the aqueous Corpufcles,
and meved up and down with them.. But
where thefe Corpufcles are not thus .con-
nected with the Water, a Number of them
join together, and form either a grofs, ora. -
. leoíe, chaffy, and fpungy Matter.

When thefe compound Particles are di-
luted with a fmell Quantity of an aqueous
Fluid, they feel flippery, clammy, and
gnctuous to the Touch, and affect the [afte
with a kind of ropy Sweetnefs. And when
& proper Quantity of. the Fluid is added, a
Commotion is prefently excited, and after-
. wards a fubtile Separation,

This Commotion and Separation fict bes
gins in the whole Subftance ; for before the
Addition of Water, the Subject may zeraàin.
in dry, folid, and large Pieces, as in’ Malt;
Sugar, Cr. which. being reduced to Pow:
der, each Grain thereof is an Aggregate of
many {maller compound Corpuícles ; thefe
being put into Water, diffolve, and fepa-
rately float therein, till at length, they bed

. t zu ] . an com¢

3o . 44 Complete Sy ftom
come fo fmall as to be invifible, and only
thicken the Confiftence of the Liquor.

. Thefe Corpufcles being thus feparated
from one another, there next enfues a Sepa-
fation of their component Particles ; that
is, the Salt, the Oil, and the Earth, are di-
vided. by the Interpofition of the aqueous
Particles, —— | ,

. 'The firft Commotion is no more thana
bare Solution ; for the faline Particles being
eafily diffolvable in Water, they are imme-
diately laid hold of by the aqueous Particles,
and carried about with them. But the fuc-
ceeding Separation, or fermentative Motion,
is a very different thing; for by this the
faline Particles are divided from thofe of
Oil and Earth, partly by the Impulfe of the.
others in their Motion, and partly by the
Force of the aqueous Particles, which are
